<meta>This frontispiece serves as a symbolic bridge between the preceding biblical text of John 4 and the deeper esoteric themes of the manuscript. **Image Description:**
A circular emblem rich in alchemical and spiritual meaning. In the center, set against a deep blue sky, is a white pelican "in its piety"—an image of a mother bird piercing her own breast to feed her chicks with her blood. The pelican stands upon a silver crescent moon. Below the moon lies a white square containing a diagonal cross (a saltire) with four red roses tucked into the corners. At the top of the scene, a golden sun shines brightly, casting its light over the entire arrangement. The image is framed by a green circular border, and the title "HERMETIC PHILOSOPHY" is inscribed around the emblem.

->HERMETIC PHILOSOPHY <note>original Latin: "PHILOSOPHIA HERMETICA." This refers to a mystical and philosophical tradition that seeks to understand the hidden laws of nature and the divine, often through the lens of alchemy.</note><-

**Key Concepts:**
*   **Hermetic Philosophy** <term>Hermeticism: A philosophical system based on ancient writings that emphasize "as above, so below," suggesting that the human soul reflects the structure of the entire universe.</term>
*   **Pelican in its piety** <note>A powerful symbol of self-sacrifice. In Christian art, it represents Christ's sacrifice; in alchemy, it represents the "rubedo" or the final stage of transformation where life is nourished by the essence of the spirit.</note>
*   **Alchemy** <term>Alchemy: Though often associated with turning lead into gold, in this context it refers to "spiritual alchemy"—the purification and perfection of the human soul.</term>
*   **Hermeticism**
*   **Rosicrucianism** <note>A secret spiritual movement that used the "Rose Cross" symbol (as seen in the emblem's square and roses) to represent the union of the earthly and the divine.</note>
*   **Emblem** <note>A symbolic picture accompanied by a motto, used as a teaching tool for moral or spiritual lessons.</note>

<summary>This frontispiece presents a complex alchemical emblem titled "Hermetic Philosophy," using the symbol of the pelican and the rose-cross to introduce the manuscript's mystical themes. It represents the intersection of traditional Christian sacrifice and the esoteric search for spiritual perfection.</summary>

<keywords>Hermeticism, alchemy, Rosicrucianism, Pelican in its piety, spiritual transformation, symbolism, frontispiece</keywords>

## To the Devout Reader.

<margin>15-264</margin>
When I was still living in the service of the Most Serene House of Saxe-Gotha <note>The Ernestine Duchy of Saxe-Gotha-Altenburg was a significant Lutheran territory in Germany where Neumann served before moving to Breslau.</note> during my time as the Court Chaplain of Altenburg <term>Hof-Prædicatur: An official position as a preacher or chaplain to a royal or noble court</term>, I was in a company where we spoke of the almost indescribable multitude of even our own prayer books. And during this conversation, it occurred to me what Solomon did when he saw, even in his own time, that there was no end to the making of books. * He quickly made a short extract from all books of teaching, which contained the **core** of all doctrine in a few words: **Fear God, and keep His commandments; for this is for all people: for that is the whole duty of man**, etc. I thought, therefore, whether a very small and short extract might not also be made from all prayer books; indeed in few words, but yet for all people, in every concern, and for all times; which one might perhaps call the

---

* Ecclesiastes XII. 12. 13. <note>original: "Predig. XII. 12. 13." In German Bibles, the Book of Ecclesiastes is titled "Der Prediger Salomo" (The Preacher Solomon).</note>

<summary>Caspar Neumann explains the origin of this book, recalling a conversation at the court of Saxe-Gotha about the overwhelming number of prayer books, which inspired him to create a concise "core" of prayer modeled after Solomon’s summary of faith in Ecclesiastes.</summary>
<keywords>Saxe-Gotha, Altenburg, Solomon, Ecclesiastes 12:12, brevity in prayer, Lutheran devotion, Caspar Neumann</keywords>

core of all prayers. And because I was prompted / for the sake of a certain person to do something of the kind / I attempted it / according to the ability / that **God** granted. And these were the thoughts / that I had in the process. I saw / that amidst the almost infinite multitude of our prayer-books / for the most part, people have the habit / of using a manner of speaking that is quite rich, verbose, and thoroughly complex, and also sometimes quite heavy in its phrasing. For example: To wrestle with death <note>This refers to the "death-struggle" or agony, a central focus of 17th-century "Ars Moriendi" (the art of dying) literature.</note> / that is without doubt a labor / so bitter / that it can disturb all our thoughts. And our Savior himself / when he wrestled with death / although he otherwise was accustomed to pray quite at length / used very few words on the Mount of Olives <note>A reference to the Garden of Gethsemane (Matthew 26), where Jesus repeated the same prayer three times.</note> / and only ever repeated the very same words. I have, however, occasionally come across prayers / above which the title stood: *Prayer of a person who is wrestling with death*. And

# Preface.

devotion <note>Original: *Andacht*. In this context, it refers to the focused state of worship or the internal spirit of the person praying.</note> sometimes likes to stop and start praying more than once; it is also not accustomed to binding itself strictly to words, nor can it always first study an elaborate <note>Original: *künstliche*. In early modern German, this implies something "artfully" or "skillfully" constructed, often referring to a logical or rhetorical structure that might feel forced during emotional distress.</note> order of its thoughts. I also knew very well that, on God's side, His hearing of our prayers is not bound by any of these things. Especially since the gift of prayer that we possess is never of such a measure that we could be certain that our prayer would not still lack something in perfection, order, or eloquence. And for this reason, we must not look at the importance or skill of our prayer at all, but instead always only at the merit of the Lord in whose name we pray. For all these reasons, I have in no way desired to reject or find fault with the previously mentioned prayer books, as I have described them.

Gleichwohl aber / so wuste ich doch
->also<-

<summary>Neumann explains that while God does not require perfect eloquence or order to hear a prayer, human devotion is often spontaneous and repetitive. He clarifies that he does not mean to reject other prayer books entirely, as the merit of prayer lies in Christ rather than the petitioner's skill.</summary>

# Preface.

### A 4

also on the contrary / and I knew it from experience / that not all people have received the gift / to pray much and at length and extensively. I would wish that we were all capable of this difficult art. But I say nevertheless / that we are not all capable of it. Some pray best / with very short / individual sighs <note>Original: *Seufftzern*. In 17th-century devotional literature, "sighs" or "groans" referred to brief, spontaneous, and heartfelt prayers that were often considered more sincere than long, formal orations.</note> / in which they can directly / and with few words / express what they are thinking. And these [prayers] are composed in such a way / that one’s devotion <note>Original: *Andacht*.</note> can, as it were, catch its breath again after every short interval. There are indeed people / whose devotion is like a precious balsam / which can be poured out so that it flows. They can pour out their heart like water / and swim, so to speak, in nothing but good thoughts. Psalm 119 <note>The longest chapter in the Bible (176 verses), representing sustained, lengthy meditation.</note> is not at all too long for them. But many others, by contrast / have only dry incense / which consists of individual grains / that must be thrown onto the coals bit by bit. The Psalms that are not much longer than Psalm 117 <note>The shortest chapter in the Bible (2 verses), representing brief, concise prayer.</note> are suited for...

...[suited for] such people best of all. Nevertheless, their prayer is likewise an offering of a sweet aroma <note>Original: *Opffer eines süssen Geruchs*. This is a biblical idiom (seen in Ephesians 5:2) referring to a sacrifice that is pleasing to God.</note> to our God. And even if it were nothing more than just such small sighs <note>Original: *Seuffzer*. In 17th-century devotional writing, "sighs" were brief, spontaneous prayers—sometimes just a few words—intended to keep the soul connected to God during daily chores.</note>: *God be gracious and merciful to me, a poor sinner! Lord help! Lord, let all things prosper!* <note>These are echoes of the "Jesus Prayer" and Psalm 118:25.</note> and the like. Indeed, a short prayer spoken entirely with devotion is much better than a long one said halfway without devotion.

And to say rightly what I am thinking: I believe the latter of these two kinds of people <note>Meaning those who pray briefly and simply.</note> are the most common in the world. But I consider the former <note>Meaning those capable of long, sustained prayer like "precious balsam."</note> to be something quite rare, and I always count it as much as a miracle <note>Original: *Wunder-Werck*.</note> whenever one finds such a person. I well hear it said: Isaiah, Daniel, and Christ himself often prayed very extensively. I also know many people who like to write long prayers; and I see even more of those who read them. I cannot look into anyone's heart to see how they pray. <margin>(a) 1 Corinthians 12:4. # Preface.

...thing is impossible. <note>Continuing from the previous page's citation of Luke 1:37: "For with God nothing is impossible."</note> However, I am speaking now of no <term>prophets</term> upon whom the Spirit of the Lord rested so powerfully that they far surpassed other men in many things. Much less am I speaking of <term>Christ</term>; rather, only of ordinary <term>Christians</term> <note>Original: *gemeinen Christen*. In this context, "ordinary" refers to the general layperson or believer as opposed to the exceptional figures of the Bible.</note>. And if every one of these would examine themselves, there would perhaps be very few who could speak with the foundation of truth: that the true zeal of their <term>devotion</term> <note>Original: *Andacht*. This word describes the internal state of being focused on and surrendered to God during prayer.</note> lasts as long as their sometimes long-winded words, or the long prayer which they recite with their mouth.

Since this devotion—which, alas, becomes weary all too soon—is the first reason why long, rambling, and especially speeches that are nowhere divided or structured <note>Original: *ab- und eingetheilte Reden*. Neumann is critiquing long, unstructured prayers that lack clear points or "breathing room," making it harder for the person praying to stay focused.</note> are not well-suited to our <term>prayer</term>; so I can also say for the second reason that such long-windedness is unnecessary when I speak with God. # Preface.

...teach them to pray. At that time, he answered them: "When (a) you pray, you shall not babble much like the <term>heathens</term> <note>Original: *Heiden*. In this context, it refers to non-Christians or pagans whose prayer rituals the author characterizes as repetitive or lacking internal substance.</note>. And he added the following reason: **Your Father knows what you need before you ask.**

If he had simply said, "you should not pray like the heathens," then I would also believe what is commonly imagined: that <term>Christ</term> <note>Original: *Christus*.</note>, in this passage, is rejecting undevout, false, hypocritical, or otherwise improperly performed <term>prayer</term> <note>Original: *Bethen*.</note>. But no. He is not speaking here generally about everything that was evil in the heathens' prayer, nor does he mention these or similar other faults in particular with a single word. Furthermore, the reason for the prohibition would not fit that explanation—that "your Father knows what you need before you ask him." Indeed, perhaps his <term>disciples</term> <note>Original: *Jünger*.</note> did not even need such a reminder <note>Neumann suggests the disciples were already sincere, so Christ's warning must have been about the length and style of prayer rather than their sincerity.</note>. # Preface.

...but [that one] must not pray in a false, hypocritical, insincere, or undevout manner like a <term>heathen</term> <note>Original: *Heide*. Neumann uses this term to refer to non-Christians or pagans of the biblical era, specifically those whose prayer rituals were perceived as mechanical.</note>; they already believed that previously as <term>Jews</term> <note>Original: *Juden*.</note>. Or, if <term>Christ</term> <note>Original: *Christus*.</note> had indeed wanted to speak of such faults in prayer, he would perhaps, according to his custom, have preferred to say "like the <term>Pharisees</term>" <note>Original: *Phariseer*.</note> and not "like the heathens." For the hypocritical <term>Pharisees</term> <note>A Jewish sect during the time of Jesus, often criticized in the New Testament for outward displays of piety.</note>, who were always boasting with useless long <term>prayers</term> <note>Original: *Gebethen*.</note>, would have provided him with enough examples so that he would not have needed to look for another far away among the heathens. 

It is, therefore, something else entirely, and something specific to the heathens, of which he now speaks. Namely, their <term>babbling</term> <note>Original Greek: βαττολογεῖν (*battologein*). This term refers to speaking in a repetitive, stammering, or empty-headed way, often linked to the idea of "beating around the bush" with many words.</note>. He explicitly mentions this by name. I must therefore, in this discourse, not allow my thoughts to focus on anything else. But <term>babbling much</term> <note>Original: *viel plappern*.</note>, as it is written in the Bible, does not mean to pray without <term>devotion</term> <note>Original: *Andacht*.</note> or from a false, deceitful, hypocritical, or otherwise evil heart; rather, it only means a

<meta>catchword: Sas</meta>

<summary>Neumann distinguishes Christ's warning against "babbling" from a general warning against hypocrisy. He argues that if Christ meant to target insincerity, he would have cited the Pharisees; instead, he targeted the specific pagan habit of long-winded, repetitive speech.</summary>

[...how they should] be taught to pray. At that time he answered them: When (a) you pray, you shall not babble much like the <term>heathens</term> <note>Original: *Heiden*. The reason for the prohibition—*because your Father knows what you need before you ask him*—would also not fit <note>Original: *reimen*, literally "rhyme," meaning to align or make sense logically.</note> that interpretation. Indeed, perhaps his disciples did not even require such a reminder. They merely requested a certain form of prayer. That one

# Preface.

prayers you will encounter in the New Testament: Also among the first Christians, whose so-called Sunday <term>Collects</term> <note>From the Latin *collecta*; these are brief, formal prayers used in liturgy to "collect" the petitions of the people into a single, concise prayer.</note> or other Collects, as public church prayers of the entire congregation, were truly not long discourses (a), as one finds them nowadays in our prayer books, but were instead purely very short <term>sighs</term> <note>Original German: *Seuffzer*. This refers to "ejaculatory prayers"—short, spontaneous bursts of devotion meant to be said quickly throughout the day.</note> consisting of only a few lines.

But people shout and write everywhere: "Nevertheless, there are also many long Psalms and other similar lengthy prayers in the Old Testament." This is true. However, there are various things to consider regarding this. Firstly, many people make the mistake of regarding everything written in the Psalms of David as a prayer without distinction. Yet this is not the case. For there are also <term>Didactic Psalms</term> <note>Original German: *Lehr-Psalmen*. These are instructional poems intended to teach moral or theological lessons rather than to serve as a direct petition to God.</note>. And for example, the first among them all <note>Psalm 1, which describes the blessedness of the righteous and the fall of the wicked.</note> is anything but a prayer—if, at least, praying means nothing other than speaking with God.

Furthermore, many a <term>prayer-Psalm</term> <note>Original: *Beth-Psalm*. A Psalm specifically intended for use as a prayer.</note> is short enough—a good prayer and yet quite brief. On the other hand, even the very longest Psalms consist of nothing but small prayers, for they all have their measured divisions. In their original language <note>Referring to Hebrew.</note>, these divisions are sometimes marked with special letters according to the alphabet <note>A reference to acrostic Psalms, such as Psalm 119, where sections begin with successive letters of the Hebrew alphabet.</note> or in other ways. Indeed, the entire discourse throughout is always concluded in one or, at most, two short lines. As noted previously, these are "ejaculatory prayers" or brief, fervent outpourings of the soul.</note>, between which devotion always finds a stopping point where it can rest. 

This is something that scholars, to be sure, understand best through their <term>accentuation</term> <note>Original: *Accentuatione*. # Preface.

---

-> And the glory <note>Original: *Herzlichkeit*; while this literally translates to "heartiness" or "sincerity," it is used here in the context of the doxology of the Lord's Prayer where "glory" (*Herrlichkeit*) is traditionally expected.</note> <-
-> From eternity to eternity / Amen! <-

Furthermore: when Paul wrote to his Timothy <margin>(a) 1 Timothy 2:1</margin> that one should offer <term>supplications</term> <note>Original: *Bitte*.</note>, <term>prayers</term> <note>Original: *Gebeth*.</note>, <term>intercessions</term> <note>Original: *Fürbitte*.</note>, and <term>thanksgivings</term> <note>Original: *Dancksagung*.</note>—and as this is sufficiently known to the learned, it is entirely unnecessary to cite them extensively here—it can be shown that according to the opinion of both old and new interpreters, these four words provide a convenient, complete, and fitting way to bring our praying into an orderly system. This is done in such a manner that one prays either for ourselves, for the turning away of evil, or for the preservation of good; or for others; or that one gives thanks to GOD.

This has pleased me all the more because I have found that my Savior himself did not forget to observe even this division in his prayer. For he distinguished quite precisely among the seven <term>petitions</term> <note>The individual requests within the Lord’s Prayer (e.g., "Hallowed be Thy name").</note> that which is properly called "supplication" and "prayer" there, and did not mix the slightest bit of one into the other. The first four petitions are a prayer for the obtaining of all good. The last three, however, are the supplication for the turning away of all evil. Intercession is not left out; rather, for the sake of brevity, it is tucked throughout into the words **Us** and **Our**. In the conclusion, however, one simultaneously sees a thanksgiving.

<summary>The author aligns the structure of the Lord's Prayer with the four types of prayer mentioned by the Apostle Paul in the Bible, arguing that Jesus perfectly balanced requests for good, pleas against evil, intercession for others, and thanksgiving.</summary>
<keywords>1 Timothy 2:1, prayer structure, petitions, intercession, thanksgiving, Paul the Apostle, Jesus, theology of prayer</keywords>

# Preface.

<meta>catchword: Ord-</meta>

---

Finally, I have also found at last that all these qualities noted in the manner of prayer used by Christ and Paul have already long been recognized in the Church of GOD and put into general practice. For what are our Litanies <note>A litany is a form of prayer consisting of a series of petitions sung or said by the clergy with fixed responses from the congregation.</note>, and specifically the well-known one used in our churches, other than such a prayer where, briefly and simply, every concern is placed only once, in good order—indeed, following the very order set by Paul: <term>supplication</term>, <term>prayer</term>, <term>intercession</term>, and <term>thanksgiving</term>. Thus, nothing can be more ancient, more common, or better known than what I have related so far regarding the manner of praying.

And if there is anyone to whom something here seems new, he must consider that it is with him as it is with us regarding many other things: we have them in our hands and before our eyes every day, and yet do not realize we possess them, simply because we sometimes do not pay enough attention to what we have.

In consideration of all this, one can now very easily gather together the sum of my entire undertaking. Namely, because our thoughts cannot remain focused for very long during devotion <note>Original: *Andacht*.</note>, and since Christ himself considered many words in prayer to be unnecessary and gave us a very short and orderly composed Our Father <note>Original: *Vatter Unser*; the Lord's Prayer.</note> as a model, I do not necessarily reject a long, rambling prayer that lacks all

<summary>The author argues that his method of structured prayer is already reflected in traditional Church Litanies and the teachings of St. Paul. He suggests that the brevity and order of the Lord's Prayer should serve as the primary model for Christian devotion.</summary>
<keywords>Litany, Lord's Prayer, St. Paul, prayer structure, devotion, 1 Timothy 2:1, order of prayer</keywords>

# Preface.

<meta>catchword: otherwise</meta>

---

orderly, rambling prayer, provided it is spoken with sufficient reflection and devotion of the heart. However, I doubt that it can be spoken with enough thoughtfulness and devotion, and I believe that for our weak and frail devotion, it is more beneficial to pray briefly and in an orderly fashion. Thus, I by no means reject long, devout persistence in prayer; but I do reject the unnecessary verbosity in the language of prayer that exceeds the measure of human devotion.

I do not speak against the long hours of prayer that many a pious Christian is accustomed to keeping in his chamber; but only against those long prayers which we put on like a robe <note>Original: *Thalar*; a long, ecclesiastical gown or academic robe.</note> that is too long for us, and the end of which we drag through the mud. You may pray as long as you wish: but even if your hour of prayer lasts longer than a single Our Father <note>Original: *Vatter Unser*; the Lord's Prayer.</note>, you should nevertheless apply yourself to such prayers that do not last much longer than one Our Father. I say once again with Chrysostom <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD), an influential Early Church Father and Archbishop of Constantinople, known for his teaching on prayer.</note>, one should use the kind of prayers where, little by little, space and rest are granted to the mind after a short interval.

With all this in mind, I previously decided—following the guidance of Christ and Paul—to compose such a general litany or prayer which would be especially useful for a person who prefers to pray briefly, and so small that one could always have it with them, even when one...

<summary>The author advocates for short, structured prayers over long, rambling ones, arguing that brief petitions better suit human frailty and maintain genuine devotion. He introduces his own "general litany" designed to be portable and concise, modeled after the Lord's Prayer and the teachings of the Church Fathers.</summary>
<keywords>prayer length, devotion, Lord's Prayer, John Chrysostom, spiritual discipline, litany, brevity in prayer</keywords>

# Preface.

<sig>B</sig>

<meta>catchword: ...ly</meta>

...otherwise cannot or does not wish to carry much with them. I have therefore prescribed the following rules for myself in my work:

1. As much as possible, to collect all the matters <note>Original: *Angelegenheiten*; referring to the various topics, concerns, or subjects of prayer.</note> found in our prayer books.

2. To include each thing that is truly the same only once, and no more.

3. To not leave everything mixed together, as is commonly done otherwise, but to bring it into a specific order as best as possible. And indeed in such a way that the first main division would be made according to the guidance of the words of Paul <note>A reference to 1 Timothy 2:1, which distinguishes between supplications, prayers, intercessions, and thanksgivings.</note>: into a **petition**, where all evil shall stand which we wish to pray away; into a **prayer**, in which all good things shall be which we wish to attain; into an **intercession**, to which the necessities of our neighbors belong; and then a **thanksgiving** for all the benefits we have received. The specific arrangement of each piece within these four parts should be made according to the way the nature of the matter and the method of teaching it in our schools <note>The author refers to the pedagogical methods (the *Art in unsren Schulen*) used in contemporary religious education to categorize theological concepts.</note> requires for each one.

4. Finally, all of this should be framed according to the style of the **Our Father** <note>Original: *Vatter Unser*; the Lord's Prayer, used here as a model for brevity and clarity.</note> in as few, clear, and plain words as possible! Also, according to the nature of the subjects: the speech should be concluded here and there and well-structured. Thus, that with this final-

<summary>The author outlines four rules for his prayer book: comprehensiveness without repetition, a four-part structure based on the Pauline categories of prayer, and a concise style modeled after the Lord's Prayer.</summary>

# Preface.

---

My prayers as well would be—according to Bartholomew's proverb <note>This likely refers to a contemporary adage or a historical reference to the apocryphal "Gospel of Bartholomew," which was sometimes characterized as being paradoxically both extensive in its spiritual scope and concise in its delivery.</note> regarding the Gospel of Christ—long and short. They are long for those who pray much in a single breath, and who will hopefully find enough material here if they wish to proceed steadily from the beginning to the end. But they are also short for those who desire only small **sighs** <term>Sighs (German: *Seuffzer*) were a popular form of short, spontaneous "ejaculatory" prayers in early modern devotion, intended to be a quick lifting of the soul to God during a busy day.</term> or who like to pause often in their prayers. These individuals, because of the paragraphs and divisions made throughout this work, will be able to begin and end where they please, resting for the length of one or several **Lord’s Prayers** <note>Original: *Vatter Unsers*. Here, the Lord's Prayer is used as a measurement of time for a meditative pause.</note>, and then proceeding again as their devotion <note>Original: *Andacht*; referring to the practitioner's internal state of religious focus and fervor.</note> leads them. This much is provided for the instruction of those who require it.

<page-num>2</page-num>

->*John of Damascus.*<- <note>John of Damascus (c. 675–749 AD) was a Syrian monk and theologian. His definition of prayer as the "raising of the mind to God" became the standard definition used by Christian scholars for centuries.</note>

Prayer is a lifting up of the mind to GOD, <note>original Latin: "Oratio est mentis in DEUM elevatio"</note> through which (1) we either pray against evils, (2) or ask for good things for (3) ourselves and others, (4) or we bless God.

Praying is an uplifting of the soul <term>The German *Gemüthes* refers to the seat of emotions, thoughts, and will—the inner heart or soul.</term> to God / where we (1) either pray away what is evil / (2) or request what is good for (3) ourselves and others / or (4) laud and praise God.

The First occurs in the **Supplication**. <note>original: *Bitte*—focused on the removal of "evil" or suffering.</note>
The Second in the **Prayer**. <note>original: *Gebeth*—here used specifically for requesting "good things" for one's own needs.</note>
The Third in the **Intercession**. <note>original: *Fürbitte*—requesting blessings for other people.</note>
The Fourth in the **Thanksgiving**. <note>original: *Dancksagung*—the act of praising and blessing God's name.</note>

<term>Heart (Hertz): In the 17th-century context, the heart was seen as the seat of the will and the center of one's moral character.</term> <term>Skill (Geschicklichkeit): Human cleverness or capability; the author views self-reliance as a spiritual trap.</term> <term>Gifts (Gaben): Abilities or advantages that should ideally lead to gratitude toward God rather than personal pride.</term> <term>Power (Gewalt): Authority or influence, which the writer recognizes as a source of potential corruption.</term> <term>Office (Amt): One’s specific calling or vocation in the social hierarchy; staying within one's "Amt" was a key virtue of the era.</term> <term>Petition (Bitte): A formal humble request made to a higher power.</term> <term>Humility (Demut): The core virtue being sought here, defined by a modest view of one's own importance and submission to God's order.</term>

<summary>This page concludes a series of petitions against pride, specifically focusing on the dangers of self-importance, the misuse of authority, and the failure to respect the boundaries of one's own social or professional station.</summary>
<keywords>pride, humility, social order, vocation, self-reliance, authority, meddling</keywords>

<term>Mocking and Blaspheming (Schmähen und Lästern): Acts of verbal aggression that damage the community or insult the divine.</term>
<term>Laziness and Idleness (Faullenzen und Müssiggehen): In this period, "idleness" was considered the "devil's workshop," a dangerous state that led to all other sins.</term>
<term>Lust of the Eyes (Augen-Lust): A biblical concept (1 John 2:16) referring to the sinful desire for things one sees, often linked to vanity and materialism.</term>
<term>Greed (Geitz): More than just stinginess, this was the "root of all evil," an avaricious attachment to money.</term>
<term>Temporal things (Zeitlichen): The physical, passing world which the devout were encouraged to regard as secondary to the soul.</term>

# [Frontispiece]

<image-desc>
An intricate copperplate engraving depicting a coastal scene under a stormy sky. At the top, a banner floats amidst dark, heavy clouds with the inscription "For All." Rain falls in diagonal streaks from the clouds onto a hilly landscape on the left. In the center, a stone building with an arched doorway and a crenellated tower sits on the shoreline. Several small sailing vessels are visible: one is tossed on the choppy waves in the foreground, while others are further out at sea. On the right, a large, vertical plume of smoke or a waterspout rises toward the sky. In the lower-left foreground, a stone block is partially visible with the biblical reference "1 Kings 18:44" engraved upon it.
</image-desc>

->For All.<-

<margin>
1 Kings 18:44 <note>original: "I Reg: 18. v. 44." This refers to the Old Testament story of the prophet Elijah on Mount Carmel. After a long drought, his servant sees a "little cloud like a man's hand" rising out of the sea, which soon brings a great rain to the whole land.</note>
</margin>

<note>Vocabulary from the original German: For All (Für Alle), 1 Kings 18:44 (I Reg: 18. v. 44), Elijah (Elias), Rain (Regen), Sea (Meer), Ship (Schiff).</note>

---

**Symbolism of the Image:**
This emblem uses the biblical story of Elijah's rain cloud to represent divine grace. Just as the small cloud eventually brought life-giving rain to everyone regardless of their station, the author suggests the following meditations or truths are intended **"For All."** The inclusion of a ship tossed on waves in the foreground is a common Baroque symbol for the human soul navigating the "stormy sea" of life, seeking the shelter of the stone tower (representing faith or the Church).

<summary>This frontispiece features an allegorical engraving of a storm at sea, referencing the biblical story of Elijah to illustrate the universal nature of divine grace. It introduces the next section of the book with the title "For All."</summary>
<keywords>Frontispiece, 1 Kings 18:44, Elijah, allegory, divine grace, sea voyage, Baroque emblem</keywords>

### Psalm 103:1

Praise the Lord, my soul / and all that is within me, His holy name. Praise the Lord, my soul / and forget not / what good He has done for you.

<note>The original German uses a virgule ( / ), a diagonal slash common in early modern printing that functions like a comma or a pause for breath during a recitation or song.</note>

<term>Praise the Lord</term> <note>original: "Lobe den HErrn" — This is a call to worship directed inward, where the speaker commands their own spirit to acknowledge the divine.</note>

<term>soul</term> <note>original: "Seele" — In this devotional context, the 'soul' represents the seat of life, emotion, and the innermost essence of a person.</note>

<term>holy name</term> <note>original: "heiligen Namen" — In biblical tradition, a name is not just a label but represents the very character, presence, and power of the individual.</note>

<term>good done</term> <note>original: "Gutes gethan" — Often translated in English bibles as "benefits," this refers to the specific acts of kindness, mercy, and providence the individual has received.</note>

# Instruction.

How certain sighs <note>original: "Seuffzer"; these refer to "ejaculatory prayers," which are brief, spontaneous legal or spiritual aspirations intended to be "breathed" throughout the day</note> may be specifically selected from this little book, and in such a manner, small prayers for all kinds of special occasions can be composed or looked up.

The letters indicate the part in which one should search:

**P. Petition / Pr. Prayer / I. Intercession / T. Thanksgiving.** <note>In the original German, these are B (Bitte), G (Gebet), F (Fürbitte), and D (Dancksagung).</note>

The figures <note>numbers</note> placed alongside them indicate the section <note>original: "Gesetze"; in this context, it refers to a numbered stanza, strophe, or specific paragraph in the text</note> in each of these four parts.

### 1. Morning Prayers.

Thanksgiving for our daily preservation. **T.** from 19 until the end.

# Petition.

# Prayer.

I set before myself whatever I <term>crave<gloss>Original: *gelüstet*. This refers to the transition from a passing thought to a fixed internal desire or "lusting" after something.</gloss></term>

# Petition.

(C) 7

Ward off all temptations of the Devil / <note>original: *Anfechtungen*. In a Lutheran context, this refers to profound spiritual trials or assaults where the believer feels crushed by the weight of sin and God's judgment.</note>
Check all faint-hearted thoughts of my heart /
Deliver me from the anxiety of my timid conscience / <note>A "timid" or "scrupulous" conscience was a major concern in Baroque devotional life; it refers to a state where a person is so terrified of their own sinfulness that they cannot trust in God's forgiveness.</note>

46. So that I may not perhaps
Conceal my sins like David / <note>David initially hid his adultery with Bathsheba and the murder of Uriah until confronted by the prophet Nathan. See Psalm 32:3.</note>
Deny them like Cain / <note>Cain famously responded to God's inquiry about his brother Abel by saying, "I know not: Am I my brother's keeper?" (Genesis 4:9).</note>
Excuse them like Saul / <note>King Saul repeatedly attempted to justify his disobedience to God's commands by blaming his soldiers or claiming he intended to make a sacrifice (1 Samuel 15).</note>
Lay the burden on others like Adam / <note>In Genesis 3:12, Adam attempted to shift the blame for his original sin onto Eve and, by extension, God: "The woman whom thou gavest to be with me, she gave me of the tree."</note>

# Prayer.

Open up your heart / so that it may take pity on me /
Stretch out your hand / so that it may save me /
Spread out your arms / so that they may receive me /
And grant your Holy Spirit / so that I may be eternally preserved. <note>The German *erhalten* suggests being kept safe or sustained in faith through eternity.</note>

2. I lie before you in my prayer / not relying on my righteousness / but on your mercy. <note>The speaker acknowledges the "Theology of the Cross," suggesting that human merit (*Gerechtigkeit*) is insufficient for salvation compared to God's compassion (*Barmhertzigkeit*).</note>
I have indeed been able to provoke you to anger; but I have nothing with which I can reconcile you / <note>The speaker admits they have the power to sin and cause offense (*erzürnen*), but lack any currency or "work" to fix the broken relationship (*versöhnen*) on their own.</note>

<term>Righteousness</term> (Gerechtigkeit): Moral perfection or the state of being "right" before God’s law.

# Prayer.

I have indeed lost my **righteousness**; but you have nonetheless kept your **love** / <note>The German *Gerechtigkeit* refers to a state of being "right" or "just" before God. The speaker admits they can no longer claim moral perfection.</note>

My **sins** are many; but with you is **forgiveness** /

# Prayer.

His **obedience** for my **transgressions** /

# Prayer.

8. Seek me as your **lost sheep** / <note>A reference to the Parable of the Lost Sheep (Luke 15:3-7), where the shepherd leaves the ninety-nine to find the one that is lost.</note>

# Prayer.

## (D) 2

Direct my heart toward **true
repentance** / <note>In the 17th and 18th centuries, "true repentance" (*wahre Busse*) was a central theological concept involving deep internal sorrow for sin and a turning toward God, distinguished from mere outward ritual.</note>
Grant me what I should **think** /
Teach me what I should **do** /

# Prayer.

And thus make of me an entirely new person / who may live eternally before God in true holiness and righteousness. <note>original: *rechtschaffener Heiligkeit und Gerechtigkeit*. In 17th-century Lutheran theology, this refers to the "New Man," a state where the believer is transformed by the Holy Spirit to live a life that reflects God's will.</note>

Recognize [my sin] like David / <note>Referring to King David’s recognition of his guilt in Psalm 51 after being confronted by the prophet Nathan.</note>
Confess [it] like the prodigal son / <note>The "lost son" from Jesus's parable (Luke 15) who returns home admitting his unworthiness.</note>
Repent [of it] like Manasseh / <note>King Manasseh of Judah was famous for turning from extreme idolatry to deep, humble repentance while in captivity.</note>

<term>Holiness</term> (Heiligkeit): The state of being set apart for God; here, it refers to the ethical living that follows forgiveness.

<term>Tax collector</term> (Zöllner): Historically, tax collectors were social outcasts in the New Testament; in prayer books, they represent the model of sincere, humble confession.
<term>Comfort</term> (Trost): A central theme in this period, meaning the spiritual reassurance and peace that comes from knowing one's sins are forgiven through Christ.

# Intercession.

->(For those who join us in confession.)<- <note>In the Lutheran tradition of this period, "confession" (Beichte) often involved a communal preparation before receiving the Eucharist, where the congregation would acknowledge their sins together before the pastor.</note>

<term>Intercession</term> (Fürbitte): The act of praying to God on behalf of other people.
<term>Confession</term> (Beichte): The formal practice of acknowledging one's sins, usually followed by a declaration of forgiveness (absolution) by a minister.

<term>Penitent</term> (bußfertig): Feeling or showing sorrow and regret for having done wrong; being ready to make amends.

# Thanksgiving.

->(Praise for the grace and mercy of God toward sinners.)<-

1. Praise the Lord, O my soul / and forget not the good things He has done for you / <note>This opening is a direct paraphrase of Psalm 103:2, a common liturgical starting point for prayers of gratitude.</note>
Who forgives all your sins / and heals all your infirmities:
Who redeems your life from destruction / and crowns you with grace and mercy. <note>original: *Verderben*. In this period, "destruction" or "corruption" refers both to the physical decay of the body and the spiritual danger of eternal loss.</note>

2. Oh Lord! How gracious and merciful /
How long-suffering and patient / <note>original: *langmüthig*. This term specifically describes God's "long-suffering" nature—His willingness to delay judgment to give sinners time to repent. These attributes are drawn from Exodus 34:6.</note>

<term>Thanksgiving</term> (Dancksagung): A formal prayer or hymn expressing gratitude to God for His attributes and actions.
<term>Grace</term> (Gnade): The unmerited favor and love of God toward humanity.
<term>Mercy</term> (Barmherzigkeit): Compassion or forbearance shown to an offender or subject.
<term>Sinner</term> (Sünder): A person who has transgressed divine law; here, the speaker uses the term to acknowledge their need for God's forgiveness.
<term>Soul</term> (Seele): The spiritual essence of the person, called upon here to lead the act of worship.
<term>Destruction</term> (Verderben): Spiritual or physical ruin.
<term>Long-suffering</term> (langmüthig): Slow to anger; patient endurance.
<term>Patient</term> (gedultig): To be steadfast and calm under provocation or strain.

<summary>This page opens a new section of thanksgiving, utilizing the language of Psalm 103 to praise God for His forgiveness, healing, and redemption. It focuses on the divine attributes of grace and patience toward the repentant sinner.</summary>
<keywords>thanksgiving, Psalm 103, grace, mercy, redemption, forgiveness, divine attributes</keywords>

# Thanksgiving.

I have done evil / and you have remained silent /
<note>original: *geschwiegen*. This refers to God’s divine forbearance—His decision to hold back judgment to give the sinner time to repent.</note>

<note>original: *sicher*. In historical devotional literature, "security" (Sicherheit) was a negative term meaning spiritual apathy or a false sense of safety that ignored one's need for God.</note>

<term>Conscience</term> (original: *Gewissen*): The internal moral compass; a "peaceful conscience" was a primary goal of early modern piety.
<term>Resolution</term> (original: *Vorsatz*): A commitment to a better moral path.

# Thanksgiving.

->(The joyful feeling of the regained grace of GOD.)<-

9. Oh, blessed is he / whose transgressions are forgiven / whose sin is covered!
<note>original: *Ubertrettungen... Sünde bedecket*. This is a direct translation of Psalm 32:1. In early modern piety, "covering" sin meant that God chose to no longer look upon the individual's faults because of his mercy.</note>
Blessed is the person / to whom the LORD does not attribute iniquity / in whose spirit there is no deceit.
<note>original: *Missethat nicht zurechnet... kein Falsch*. This refers to "imputation," a theological concept where God "clears the books" of a sinner's debts. "No deceit" (or guile) refers to a heart that is completely honest and open before God.</note>
How light my conscience is now once again!
<note>The author uses "lightness" to contrast with the "heavy burden" of sin described in earlier sections of the prayer book.</note>
How great is my faith!
How firm is my hope!
How fervent is my love!
<note>original: *brünstig*. In 17th-century devotional writing, this term literally means "burning" or "glowing," describing a heart that is "on fire" with divine love.</note>
How joyful is my heart!

<term>Thanksgiving</term> (original: *Dancksagung*): A structured prayer of gratitude for spiritual or physical blessings.
<term>Grace of God</term> (original: *Gnade GOTTES*): Unmerited divine favor and the central theme of this prayer.
<term>Transgressions</term> (original: *Ubertrettungen*): Violations of divine law; literally "stepping across" a boundary.
<term>Iniquity</term> (original: *Missethat*): An older term for a wicked act or a specific moral failing.
<term>Conscience</term> (original: *Gewissen*): The internal faculty that judges one's own actions; a "light" conscience was the ultimate goal of the penitent.

# Prayer.

<term>Atonement</term> (gebüsset): The act of making amends or reparation for guilt or wrongdoing; in this context, Christ's sacrificial death.
<term>Innocent Suffering</term> (unschuldiges Leiden): A central Christian concept where Jesus, though without sin, suffers the punishment intended for sinners.

# Thanksgiving.

And once again, more than what I am belongs to you, because you have redeemed me. <note>original: "erlöset" — To be ransomed or set free from a debt; the author is highlighting that the "price" paid for the soul makes it God's property twice over.</note>
Since you have now also given yourself to me,
I no longer know of anything else to think upon,
For nothing is your equal in heaven or on earth.

->(Thanksgiving for our re-<-
->demption.)<-

9. Therefore, I thank you now also, dear heavenly Father:
Not as perfectly as I should;
But only as well as I can;

<term>Redemption</term> (Erlösung): In this theological context, it refers to Christ "buying back" humanity from sin. The author views this as a second, greater claim on his life than his initial creation.
<term>Heavenly Father</term> (himmlischer Vatter): A title emphasizing the familial intimacy between the worshiper and the Divine.
<term>Given yourself</term> (dich selber... gegeben): This likely refers to the "Great Exchange" where Christ gives His life and divinity to the believer, often associated with the reception of the Sacrament.

<summary>This page concludes a prayer of gratitude, arguing that the believer owes God more than their entire existence because they have been both created and redeemed. It ends with a humble admission that human thanks can never be perfect, only sincere.</summary>
<keywords>redemption, gratitude, heavenly father, debt of love, divinity, inadequacy of man</keywords>

# Thanksgiving.

That you did not even spare your own Son, but gave him up for us all; <note>This is a paraphrase of Romans 8:32, a central verse in Lutheran theology emphasizing that God's greatest gift was the sacrifice of His Son.</note>

And that you have refreshed us with the healing gift of his body and blood. <note>original: "erquicket" — This term implies a deep spiritual restoration or reviving of the soul, specifically through the Sacrament of the Altar (Holy Communion).</note>

10. I thank you, my **Jesus**, for all your love and faithfulness:

For your **incarnation** and labor,

For your **tears** and **sighs**,

For your **agony** and **bloody sweat**,

For your **shame** and **contempt**,

<term>Incarnation</term> (Menschwerdung): The theological concept of God becoming human. The author views Christ’s entire human life—not just his death—as a gift to be thanked for.
<term>Agony</term> (Todes-Kampff): Literally a "death-struggle." In this context, it refers to the intense mental and spiritual suffering Jesus experienced in the Garden of Gethsemane.
<term>Bloody sweat</term> (blutiger Schweiß): A reference to the account in the Gospel of Luke (22:44) where Jesus's sweat became like drops of blood, symbolizing the weight of the world's sins.
<term>Body and Blood</term> (Leib und Blut): The components of the Eucharist. The author describes them as a "healing gift" (heilsame Gabe), suggesting that the ritual provides both spiritual medicine and strength.

<summary>This page continues a litany of gratitude, moving from the Father’s sacrifice to a direct address to Jesus, listing the specific physical and emotional sufferings he endured for humanity. It highlights the believer's spiritual refreshment through the Sacrament of his body and blood.</summary>
<keywords>Jesus Christ, incarnation, Eucharist, Passion of Christ, Gethsemane, spiritual healing, gratitude</keywords>

# Thanksgiving.

For your beatings and wounds,

For your pains and anguish, <note>In this period, "Angst" referred to intense spiritual distress or the "terrors of hell" rather than simple modern anxiety.</note>

For your cross and death.

11. I thank You,

For the forgiveness of sins,

For peace with God,

For the rest of my conscience, <note>original: "Ruhe meines Gewissens" — This refers to the spiritual peace that follows confession and absolution, where the believer no longer feels the "stings" of guilt or the weight of divine judgment.</note>

For the joy of eternal life,

And for all other benefits of your **redemption**, which you have earned for me through your blood.

->12. LORD<-

<term>Redemption</term> (Erlösung): Literally a "ransoming" or "setting free." It refers to the theological belief that Jesus "purchased" humanity's freedom from sin and death through his suffering.
<term>Wounds</term> (Wunden): A major focus of early modern devotion, where the physical injuries of Jesus were contemplated as a source of healing and a place of refuge for the sinner.
<term>Conscience</term> (Gewissen): In this tradition, the conscience is the internal space where a person encounters God's Law. A "resting" or "quiet" conscience is considered the greatest earthly treasure, achievable only through Christ’s merit.

<summary>This page concludes a litany of gratitude for the Passion of Christ, listing the physical suffering he endured and the spiritual benefits—such as peace of conscience and eternal life—granted to the believer in return.</summary>

# Thanksgiving.

12. LORD! I will praise your name as long as I live /

In you I will remain /
In you I will rejoice /
For you I will live /

<term>Doxology</term>: The final verse functions as a doxology—a short hymn of praise to the Holy Trinity (Father, Son, and Holy Spirit) that traditionally concludes Christian prayers.

### Key Vocabulary from this Page:
**Petition** (Bitte): A formal request or prayer addressed to God.
**Holy Supper** (Abendmahl): The sacrament of the Eucharist.
**Salvation** (Heil): Spiritual rescue from sin and its consequences.

**Body** (Leib): The physical body of Christ received in the bread of the sacrament.

Keep my family (my wife, children, parents, siblings, and all others) healthy.
Govern those with whom I shall interact today toward nothing but goodness /
And finally, have mercy on all your **creatures** <term>creatures</term> (Creaturen): A term referring to all living things created by God, emphasizing the interconnectedness of the natural world. / Amen.

### Petition.

### Prayer.

1. Lord God Father, Son, and Holy Spirit, in your name I go to sleep; so let now also your poor creature—my body, my soul, my belongings and goods—be commended to your goodness. For I live for you. ### Decorative Endpaper

*The page is covered in an ornate floral pattern set against a vibrant green background. The stylized botanical design includes blossoms and foliage in shades of pink, purple, orange, and yellow. Some of the floral elements are detailed with intricate cross-hatched and dotted textures, which add a sense of depth and hand-crafted quality to the paper.*

<term>Buntpapier: A German term for "decorated paper." During the 18th century, these papers—which could be marbled, block-printed, or hand-painted—were prized as decorative elements for the inside covers and flyleaves of high-quality bookbindings.</term>

<note>This sequence of letters is likely an acronym for a religious phrase or a personal dedication. In this period, owners of prayer books often inscribed their initials or a favorite prayer in abbreviated form. While the exact phrase is not identified, the pattern of "F" and "H" often points toward German phrases involving "Friede" (Peace) or "Heiland" (Savior).</note>

### Illustration of the Rescue of Saint Peter

*This intricate illustration is crafted using <term>Straw Marquetry: A decorative technique where flattened, dyed pieces of straw are inlaid onto a surface to create patterns or pictures.</term> The scene is framed by a rich border of acanthus leaves and floral designs, with draped curtains at the top, creating a theatrical or "staged" appearance common in 18th-century religious art.*

->Matthew 14: Chapter<-
<note>original: "Matthæi 14. C."</note>

->O Lord, help me<-
<note>original: "O Herr hilff mir"</note>

In the center of the composition, Jesus stands upon the turbulent surface of the water, wearing a vibrant red cloak. He reaches out his hand to pull Saint Peter from the waves. Peter is depicted kneeling on the water, his face turned upward toward Jesus in a plea for salvation.

To the left, the artist has included a peaceful shoreline featuring a tree, lush foliage, and two small ducks swimming near the bank. In the distance, a small sailing vessel carries two other figures, likely the remaining disciples, who witness the miracle from the safety of the boat. The sky is meticulously rendered with horizontal strips of straw to suggest a vast, atmospheric horizon.
